An infrared orthophoto displays a mountainous landscape in Kebnekaise,
Sweden, featuring prominent white areas representing glaciers and
perpetual snow. The surrounding terrain appears in shades of dark
reddish-brown, characteristic of bare rock or sparse vegetation in an
infrared false-colour composite. Some brighter red patches within the
reddish-brown areas may indicate more vigorous vegetation. The rugged
topography shows valleys and mountain slopes. The image credit is
“Lantmäteriet, Sweden.”

**Methodological advice:**

- Use CLC class 3.3.5 as orientation.
- The identification of glaciers and permanent snow fields depends on an
  appropriate date of the image with a low cloud coverage since clouds
  can be lead to misinterpretations due to very similar appearance in
  the satellite images.

## 7. Wetland

According to Article 1.1 of the Ramsar Convention[^22] (1971), wetlands
are: “areas of marsh, fen, peatland or water, whether natural or
artificial, permanent or temporary, with water that is static or
flowing, fresh, brackish or salt, including areas of marine water the
depth of which at low tide does not exceed six meters”. This class
refers only to inland freshwater/saline wetlands.

Inland wetlands are predominantly water-logged specific plant and animal
communities supporting water regulation and peat-related processes. This
class includes natural or modified mires, bogs and fens, as well as peat
extraction sites[^23]. Surfaces of temporary water are included in
wetlands. According EUNIS guidelines (see table below), water-logged
means the presence of the water table at or above ground level for at
least half of the year.

Adaptation of water interpretation in EUNIS[^24] and related PA classes

| PRESENCE OF WATER | PA CLASSIFICATION |
|----|----|
| Always dry (except extraordinary floods) | 1 Urban (except ponds related to industrial units); 2 Croplands (except rice fields); 3 Woodland and forest (except swamp forests; 4 Grassland, 5 Heathland and scrub; 6.1 Sparsely vegetated areas; 6.3.1.1 Bare rocks, outcrops, cliffs; 6.3.2 Burnt areas |
| Permafrost | N/A |
| Ice | 6.3.3.1 Glaciers |
| Permanent snow | 6.3.3.2 Perpetual snow |
| Mesic, moist, or humid | 3 Woodland and forest 4.2.x Natural and semi-natural grasslands |
| Water-logged (the water table at or above ground level for at least half of the year). Wet soil (including periods with presence of dry soils in dry regions). | 3 Woodland and forest 7. Wetland |
| Permanently covered by water table, but water level variations occurs (including occasionally periods with presence of wet soils) | 8.1.1 Natural & semi-natural water courses; 8.1.3 Seasonally connected water courses (oxbows); 8.2 Lakes and reservoirs (except temporary water bodies); 8.3.3 Marine inlets and fjords; 7.2.3 Intertidal flats; 8.3.1 Lagoons; 8.3.2. Estuaries |
| Permanently covered by water with tidal movements | 8.4.1 Open sea 8.4.2 Coastal waters |

A decision criteria tree is established to clarify guideline
distinctions between the sub-classes as follows:

<img
src="Protected_Areas_2021-2024_Nomenclature_Guideline_v1-media/img-5e0cdbfd455e1bf54e2fbd0de47f6d88.png"
data-fig-alt="This classification workflow diagram illustrates the decision process for identifying various wetland types, starting with general environmental conditions and leading to specific land cover or land use categories. The process begins by determining if an area is &#39;Inland?&#39;. 1. If the area is NOT Inland, the classification outcome is &#39;7.1.1 Inland marshes&#39;. 2. If the area IS Inland, the next check is &#39;Wet soil?&#39;. a. If the soil is NOT Wet, the classification outcome is &#39;7.1.1 Inland marshes&#39;. b. If the soil IS Wet, the area is confirmed as a &#39;7. Wetland&#39; (the primary classification step). i. From &#39;7. Wetland&#39;, a decision is made based on &#39;Salinity?*&#39;. 1. If &#39;Salinity?&#39; is NO: A. The process then checks for the presence of &#39;Mosses, dwarf shrub vegetation and herbaceous vegetation typical for hummock mires, lawn and carpet mires, mud-bottom mires?&#39;. * If YES, the area is classified as &#39;Peat bog&#39;. * If the &#39;Peat bog&#39; is &#39;Exploited?&#39; (YES), the final classification is &#39;7.1.2.1 Exploited peat bogs&#39;. * If the &#39;Peat bog&#39; is &#39;Exploited?&#39; (NO), the final classification is &#39;7.1.2.2 Unexploited peat bogs&#39;. * If NO to the vegetation description, the final classification is &#39;7.1.1 Inland marshes&#39;. 2. If &#39;Salinity?&#39; is YES: A. The area is identified as &#39;Saline marshes&#39;. B. A further check is made: &#39;Exploited?&#39;. * If &#39;Exploited?&#39; is NO, the final classification is &#39;7.1.1 Inland marshes&#39;. * If &#39;Exploited?&#39; is YES, the final classification is &#39;1.3.1 Mineral extraction&#39;." />

This classification workflow diagram illustrates the decision process
for identifying various wetland types, starting with general
environmental conditions and leading to specific land cover or land use
categories.

The process begins by determining if an area is “Inland?”. 1. If the
area is NOT Inland, the classification outcome is “7.1.1 Inland
marshes”. 2. If the area IS Inland, the next check is “Wet soil?”. a. If
the soil is NOT Wet, the classification outcome is “7.1.1 Inland
marshes”. b. If the soil IS Wet, the area is confirmed as a “7. Wetland”
(the primary classification step). i. From “7. Wetland”, a decision is
made based on “Salinity?*”. 1. If ”Salinity?” is NO: A. The process then
checks for the presence of ”Mosses, dwarf shrub vegetation and
herbaceous vegetation typical for hummock mires, lawn and carpet mires,
mud-bottom mires?”.* If YES, the area is classified as”Peat bog”. \* If
the “Peat bog” is “Exploited?” (YES), the final classification is
“7.1.2.1 Exploited peat bogs”. \* If the “Peat bog” is “Exploited?”
(NO), the final classification is “7.1.2.2 Unexploited peat bogs”. \* If
NO to the vegetation description, the final classification is “7.1.1
Inland marshes”. 2. If “Salinity?” is YES: A. The area is identified as
“Saline marshes”. B. A further check is made: “Exploited?”. \* If
“Exploited?” is NO, the final classification is “7.1.1 Inland marshes”.
\* If “Exploited?” is YES, the final classification is “1.3.1 Mineral
extraction”.

\*To determinate the salinity of wetlands it is necessary to use
ancillary data.

### 7.1 Inland wetlands

#### 7.1.1 Inland marshes

**Definition:**

Inland wetlands without a direct connection to the open ocean with
significant content of water, which is influenced by a certain seasonal
fluctuation.

This category corresponds to 4.1.1 CORINE Land Cover class (Inland
marshes). Both inland freshwater marshes and inland salt marshes are
included in this class. They are created where saline ground water rises
to the surface, or in endorheic basins.

Inland marshes appear with or without a vegetation coverage. The
coverage usually consists of reeds. Reed is the common name for several
tall, grass-like plants of wetlands and rivers. They are all members of
the order Poales: *Poaceae*, *Cyperaceae*, *Sparganiaceae*, *Typhacea*
or *Restionaceae*.

Reed in marshes groups basically two types of plants:

- Sedges (*Cyperaceae* family) are monocotyledon plants with solid and
  triangular stems; 3-ranked leaves; with flowers, and lenticular or
  triangular fruits. Sedges are common in wet habitats, including
  marshes, and in tundra habitats.
- Rushes (*Juncaceae* family) are monocotyledon plants with solid and
  mostly round stems; few leaves, only basal or reduced to sheaths; with
  flowers and fruits in capsules. Rushes are common in wet habitats.

If not covered by vegetation: basically mud areas and/or waterlogged
grasses and grass-like plants will be mapped.

Inland salt marshes without vegetation are located predominantly in
Pannonic region (Pannonic salt steppes), Turkey, in Mediterranean
specific locations and in endorheic basins across Europe.

**This category includes:**

- Marshes (freshwater or salt) with or without vegetation (reeds)
  coverage.
- Areas, where reeds are harvested.
- Reed covered wetland along river sides
- In Nordic conditions: includes non-grazed/mowed and non-peat producing
  wetlands. These areas normally have a vegetation of tall grasses and
  sedges.
- Areas flooded at least six months a year with low or no vegetation.
- Grasslands highly wet or flooded at least six months a year.
- Bare soils as results of previous presence of water will be also
  included within this category.
- It will be taken into account that the Baltic Sea has only brackish
  coastal waters, which qualify for inland freshwater marshes.
- Limestone mires.
- Floating vegetation.
- Reed beds in saline marsh with coverage less than 30%.

**This category excludes:**

- Floating aquatic vegetation (species such as *Nuphar* spp., *Nymphaea*
  spp., *Potamageton* spp. And *Lemna* spp.) is not considered as reeds
  and is included in 8.2 Lakes and reservoirs or 8.4 Sea and ocean.
- Grassland highly wet or flooded less than six months a year
  $\rightarrow$ 4.1 Managed grassland / 4.2.1.x Semi natural grassland.
- Rice fields $\rightarrow$ 2.1.1 Arable land.
- Reeds mixed with trees ($> 5$m) in rivers banks $\rightarrow$ 3.x.x
  Woodland and forest.
- Open water $\geq 0.5$ ha in wetland areas (class 8.2.1 Natural lakes).
- Mesic/moist grasslands $\rightarrow$ 4.1 Managed grassland / 4.2.1.x
  Semi-natural grassland.
- Marine wetlands such as salt marshes or salines $\rightarrow$ 8.1.x
  Coastal wetlands.
- Mires with more than 30% of tree cover density are considered as 3.x.x
  Woodland and forest.
- Inland exploited salines and extraction of sodium minerals in salt
  marshes, salt lakes or other locations $\rightarrow$ 1.3.1 Mineral
  extraction, dump and construction sites.
- Shrub along river sides and on river banks (may include small patches
  of reeds with area $< 0.5$ Ha) where neither climax tree-like forest
  formations nor grassland is detected (mainly located in areas of
  Mediterranean and continental climates with a summer season with
  warm-temperate & low precipitation) $\rightarrow$ 3.4.0 Transitional
  woodland and scrub.

<div>

</div>

Wetland and semi-natural grassland distinction example:

<div>

</div>

Special case in Nordic conditions:

In the case of Nordic countries, when typical mire/peat structures
cannot be seen in the satellite image/orthographic photo, it is
difficult to determine if an area is peat producing or not. Therefore
the position in the terrain is used for guidance. Areas directly
adjacent to bigger rivers or lakes are included in 7.1 when visual
interpretation is insufficient. This is because of lower chances of peat
production when oxygen from flowing water is supplied to the ecosystem
as opposed to ecosystems adjacent to standing water.

Non-peat producing wetlands normally have a vegetation of grasses and
sedges, either grazed/mowed or not. These areas are included in 7.1.1
Inland marshes. Temporarily flooded areas with low grasses and sedges
are included in 4.2.1.2 Semi-natural grassland without woody plants.

**Methodological advice:**

- Use of Corine Land Cover for first location of main sites.
- Freshwater status of the water bodies will be determined by location,
  assuming that considerable distance (up-stream) from the sea,
  restrains the salty water from mixing into the water bodies.

#### 7.1.2 Peat bogs

##### 7.1.2.1 Exploited peat bogs

**Definition:**

Open exploited peat producing wetlands that are not greatly affected by
lakes, sea water or water from water courses. Exploited peat bogs are
characterized by linear trench and parcel structure.

**This category includes:**

- Extraction of peat in mires.

**This category excludes:**

- Abandoned peat extraction site with regrowth $\rightarrow$ Class 3.x.x
  Woodland and forest.
- Unexploited peat bogs $\rightarrow$ 7.1.2.2 Unexploited peat bog.
- Exploited peat bogs which are covered by trees, waterbodies or managed
  grassland on an area $> 0.5$ ha $\rightarrow$ 3.x.x Woodland and
  forest/8.x.x Water/4.1 Managed grassland.

The delineation of these polygons is attributed to CORINE Land Cover,
fairing with the satellite image situation. The imagery credit is ESA.

##### 7.1.2.2 Unexploited peat bogs

**Definition:**

Open unexploited peat producing wetlands that are not greatly affected
by lakes, sea water or water from water courses. In Nordic conditions
this class is normally a heterogeneous vegetation type where mire
vegetation dominates in a mosaic of heath vegetation, alpine grassland,
alpine willow bushes and a rocky ground. Alternative names are proposed
for this class as ”Peat producing land” or “Peat bog and other peat
producing land”.

**This category includes:**

- The mire types hummock, lawn, carpet mires and mud bottom mires.
- Peat bogs in Alpine / Sub-Alpine environment across Europe.
- Mosaics of complex distribution between the classes 7.1.2.2
  Unexploited peat bog and 8.2.1 Natural lakes, where the class 7.1.2.2
  covers at least 70% of the surface.

**This category excludes:**

- Areas with $>10$ % tree cover $\rightarrow$ Class 3.x.x Woodland and
  forest.
- Peat extraction site $\rightarrow$ 7.1.2.1 Exploited peat bog.

<img
src="Protected_Areas_2021-2024_Nomenclature_Guideline_v1-media/img-754f78c000e44d2893d78a126bcc8a44.jpeg"
style="width:308pt"

A ground-level photograph depicts an exploited peat bog landscape in
Roscommon, Ireland, featuring rolling hills in the background under a
light, overcast sky. The foreground and midground consist of diverse
vegetation, including patches of green grass and darker, reddish-brown
heathland or shrubbery, characteristic of a moorland environment. A
narrow, unpaved track or road is faintly visible winding into the
distance on the right side of the midground, with several scattered
rocks visible in the same area. The image credit states “©European
Union, 2012 LUCAS”.

**Appearance:**

<div>

</div>

In low lying areas or local depressions complex mire types include areas
of open peat, small ponds and streaks of mosses/heath vegetation.

**Methodological advice:**

- Use of CLC class 4.1.2 as orientation.

### 7.2 Coastal wetlands

#### 7.2.1 Salt marshes

**Definition:**

Coastal salt marshes, according to the EUNIS habitat description, are
dominated by sediments and salt-tolerant stands of vegetation, occurring
on the extreme upper shore of sheltered coasts and periodically covered
by high tides. According to the definition of CLC class 5.2, Marine
waters are oceanic and continental shelf waters, bays and narrow
channels including sea lochs, fiords and straits.

The class salt marshes includes according to EUNIS following habitat
types: salt marsh drift lines, upper salt marshes, mid-upper salt
marshes and saline brackish reed, rush and sedge beds and littoral
sediments not included in 7.2.3 Intertidal flats or 6.2.1 Beaches and
dunes.

The Estuaries class (8.3.2) includes marine waters not included in class
8.4.0 Sea and ocean or other lagoons and coastal wetlands classes.

Vegetated low-lying areas, above the high-tide line, susceptible to
flooding by seawater. Often in the process of filling in, gradually
being colonized by salt tolerant plants (saline reed beds). Salt marshes
also appear without or less vegetation coverage.

On the date of the EO data of reference these areas may be covered or
not by water. In this category, the entire marsh area will be considered
as class 7.2.1, regardless if it is covered by water or not on the date
of the image.

**This category includes:**

- Vegetated beds in salt marshes (basically salt-tolerant plants but
  also sedges and rushes).
- Salt marshes in estuaries and delta areas with or without vegetation
  coverage.
- Floating aquatic vegetation in coastal zones.
- Muddy areas covered by water in coastal salt marshes.

**This category excludes:**

- Inland salt marshes $\rightarrow$ 7.1.1 Inland marshes.
- Coastal lagoons $\rightarrow$ 8.3.1 Lagoons.
- Salines $\rightarrow$ 7.2.2 Salines.
- Intertidal flats $\rightarrow$ 7.2.3 Intertidal flats.
- Estuaries $\rightarrow$ 8.3.2 Estuaries.

<div>

</div>

**Appearance:**

<div>

</div>

In the case of salt marshes transformed to agriculture, classification
rules are applied as follows:

<div>

</div>

These rules for transformed salt marshes also apply to 8.3.1 Lagoons.

The treatment of water inside salt marshes is based on the following
rules:

- Water-logged areas (having water or not at the date of the image) are
  classified as 7.2.1 Salt marshes.
- Relevant ponds and channels visible in the image (containing water or
  clear surface depressions) will be classified as 8.3.1 Lagoons.

A false color infrared satellite image of the Pineiou Delta, a Natura
2000 site in Greece, captured on 2011-08-23 by Spot 5 at 2.5 m
resolution, using a 1/2/3 band combination. The image depicts a coastal
area featuring a large body of water on the left, rendered in bright

visible parcels and drainage channels, is identified in context as being
covered by grassland or scrubland, or heathland and scrub, rather than
active agriculture.

**Methodological advice:**

- Use of CLC class 4.2.1 as orientation.

#### 7.2.2 Salines

**Definition:**

CORINE Land Cover 422 Salines class definition is adopted: “Salt-pans,
active or in process of abandonment. Sections of salt marsh exploited
for the production of salt by evaporation. They are clearly
distinguishable from the rest of the marsh by their parcellation and
embankment systems”[^25].

Salines (active ones and in process of abandonment) will be based on
CORINE land cover and computer assisted visual interpretation.

**This category includes:**

- Exploited coastal salines.
- Salines organized to breed fish in coastal areas and any type of fish
  farms in maritime environments (including inland oyster fish farms).

**This category excludes:**

- Saline coastal lagoons $\rightarrow$ 7.2.1 Salt marshes.
- Salines not exploited (irregular forms, presence of vegetation in
  ponds, barriers covered by vegetation) will be classified in other
  classes as 8.3.1 Lagoons or 7.2.1 Salt marshes.
- Freshwater fish ponds $\rightarrow$ 8.2.3 Aquaculture ponds.
- Inland salines $\rightarrow$ 1.3.1 Mineral extraction, dump and
  construction sites. Floating fish farms $\rightarrow$ 8.4.0 Sea and
  ocean.

<div>

</div>

**Appearance:**

- Water ponds with regular shape, close quarters and many small ponds in
  or near the coastline. Different colours of ponds according to their
  level of water. Without vegetation. In many cases, associated to
  buildings.

#### 7.2.3 Intertidal flats

**Definition:**

CORINE Land Cover 423 Intertidal flats class definition is adopted:
“Generally vegetationless expanses of mud, sand or rock lying between
high and low water marks”[^26].

**This category includes:**

- Area between tide marks, basically composed by mud, rocks or boulders.

**This category excludes:**

- Coastal lagoons and marshes $\rightarrow$ 8.3.1 Lagoons / 7.2.1 Salt
  marshes.
- Estuaries (rivers meet the sea and salt water is diluted by fresh
  river water) $\rightarrow$ 8.3.2 Estuaries.
- Beaches and dunes $\rightarrow$ 6.2.x Beaches, dunes, sands.
- Transformed intertidal flats $\rightarrow$ Current land cover.

<div>

</div>

**Appearance:**

- Generally, mud flats and water channels in the coastline.

src="Protected_Areas_2021-2024_Nomenclature_Guideline_v1-media/img-acbfc730c5b4dc6d995486ad9a68771c.jpeg"

False-colour satellite map showing the Santa Pola salines, Spain,
captured on 2011-08-12 by Spot 5 (2.5 m resolution, 1/2/3 band
combination). The map depicts a coastal environment with deep blue open
water, extensive lighter blue/grey/brown intertidal flats featuring
intricate channels, and landmasses. Vegetated areas on land appear

combination common in satellite imagery. A prominent green polygon
boundary delineates a large coastal zone encompassing both terrestrial
and intertidal features, likely corresponding to a Copernicus Land

definition of intertidal flats as generally vegetationless expanses of
mud, sand, or rock between high and low water marks. The data source is
CNES 2011©, distributed by Airbus DS/Spot Image.

Difference between intertidal flats and estuaries:

Intertidal flats are characterized by the influence of the sea and the
presence of tidal channels. 8.3.2 Estuaries are characterized by the
influence of the river.

<img
src="Protected_Areas_2021-2024_Nomenclature_Guideline_v1-media/img-3436905928b6fa2163268c0814dd3a1c.jpeg"
data-fig-alt="This conceptual diagram illustrates the key geomorphological differences between Intertidal Flats and Estuaries. The diagram is divided into two panels, each representing a distinct coastal feature. The left panel, labelled &#39;INTERTIDAL FLATS&#39;, depicts a wide coastal area where a main channel of water (dark blue) branches extensively into numerous smaller tidal channels (lighter blue) within a broad intertidal zone (grey), bordered by land (green) and a coastline feature (yellow). A large white arrow points into the main channel from the seaward side, labelled &#39;SEA INFLUENCE&#39;, indicating the dominant impact of tides and seawater. The right panel, labelled &#39;ESTUARIES&#39;, shows a body of water (dark blue) where a river (grey channel at the top) empties into a larger water body, bordered by land (green) and a coastline feature (yellow). A large white arrow points from upstream into the river, labelled &#39;RIVER INFLUENCE&#39;, highlighting the predominant impact of freshwater flow from the river. The diagram visually differentiates these two land cover types based on whether the primary shaping force is tidal &#39;SEA INFLUENCE&#39; or fluvial &#39;RIVER INFLUENCE&#39;."
alt="Schematic differentiation 7.2.3 Intertidal flats/ 8.3.2 Estuaries." />

This conceptual diagram illustrates the key geomorphological differences
between Intertidal Flats and Estuaries. The diagram is divided into two
panels, each representing a distinct coastal feature.

The left panel, labelled “INTERTIDAL FLATS”, depicts a wide coastal area
where a main channel of water (dark blue) branches extensively into
numerous smaller tidal channels (lighter blue) within a broad intertidal
zone (grey), bordered by land (green) and a coastline feature (yellow).
A large white arrow points into the main channel from the seaward side,
labelled “SEA INFLUENCE”, indicating the dominant impact of tides and
seawater.

The right panel, labelled “ESTUARIES”, shows a body of water (dark blue)
where a river (grey channel at the top) empties into a larger water
body, bordered by land (green) and a coastline feature (yellow). A large
white arrow points from upstream into the river, labelled “RIVER
INFLUENCE”, highlighting the predominant impact of freshwater flow from
the river.

The diagram visually differentiates these two land cover types based on
whether the primary shaping force is tidal “SEA INFLUENCE” or fluvial
“RIVER INFLUENCE”.

**Methodological advice:**

- Use of CLC class 4.2.3 as orientation.
- Use of EU-DEM (0 m altitude line) to identify the exterior border of
  intertidal flat.
- The outer border will be defined by the supratidal zone (zone
  regularly splashed, but not submerged by sea water).
- Use of Transitional Water layer (European Framework Directive) to
  identify general limits of coastal waters.

## 8. Water

This level comprises all water courses and water bodies, which are
permanent, visible at the surface, containing freshwater and located in
the inland as well as coastal water bodies influenced by saline sea
water like lagoons, marine inlets and estuaries. Open sea is also
included in this parent class.

As general for this category, the water level visible in the EO data
used for interpretation will be mapped. Where the water is missing, the
current land cover seen in the image will be mapped. This general
principle is valid for lakes (especially dams, barrages and lakes of
hydropower stations), rivers, oxbows, fishponds, etc.

### 8.1 Water courses

#### 8.1.1 Natural & semi-natural water courses

**Definition:**

Natural stream of water that empties into another body of water or into
the sea. Also watercourses that cease to flow for part of the year,
leaving a partially dry bed or water pools (EUNIS definition class C2.5)
are included here. Different classes of temporary rivers are
considered[^27]: snowmelt and glacial meltwater; perched and
semi-perched alluvial and karstic non-permanent streams.

**This category includes:**

- Natural streams of water with more than 10 m width.
- Un- or less vegetated river beds with a width $< 10$ m along water
  courses with a width $> 10$ m.

**This category excludes:**

- Highly modified natural water courses and canals $\rightarrow$ 8.1.2
  Highly modified water courses and canals.
- Dams, barrages, lakes of hydropower stations and other water bodies
  $\rightarrow$ 8.2.1 Natural lakes.
- Water bodies belonging to the river system $\rightarrow$ 8.1.3
  Seasonally connected water courses (oxbows).
- Small rivers completely covered by vegetation (trees, scrub, hedges)
  $\rightarrow$ map current land cover.
- Bank within a river that is covered by scrub is not considered as
  inland wetlands will be classified as 3.4.0 Transitional woodland and
  scrub.
- Lakes that are connected to the river systems are mapped as 8.2.1
  Natural lakes.
- Small marinas in rivers: Marina water surface: 8.2.1 Natural lakes /
  Sports facilities around water 1.4 Green urban, sports and leisure
  facilities.
- Completely or partly dry river beds with a width of $> 10$ m
  $\rightarrow$ map whole riverbed as 6.2.2 River banks.
- Dry river bed-sections $<10$ m in width and max. 100 m in length,
  which are parts of a $> 10$ m river system $\rightarrow$ 6.2.2 River
  banks.

<div>

</div>

**Appearance:**

A false-colour infrared satellite image depicts a diverse European
landscape, likely used for land cover/land use mapping within the
Copernicus Land Monitoring Service (CLMS). The image features a wide,
winding river flowing from the upper-left to the lower-right,
characterized by a significant bend in the upper-central area. Water
bodies, including the river and two distinct lakes/reservoirs in the
upper-central part, appear as dark blue or black. Urban and built-up
settlements are prominent in bright turquoise and cyan, clustered along
the riverbanks and extending into the surrounding terrain. Dense,
healthy vegetation, such as forests and some actively growing
agricultural fields, is represented by shades of deep red and magenta.
Agricultural land consists of numerous parcels displaying varying
colours from lighter reds and pinks to teal and light green, indicating
different crop types, stages of growth, or bare soil. The image provides
a detailed view of natural watercourses, extensive forested areas, and a
patchwork of agricultural fields, illustrating the interplay of
different land cover types in the region. No specific geographic
location, scale bar, or date is visible.

Exceptions from MMU:

Exceptions from MMU $> 0.5$ ha are made for “8.1.1” in order to keep the
network formed by these linear features.

MMW exceptions:

To maintain continuity of linear features, the MMW may fall below the
limit of 10 m over a distance of up to 100 m.

#### 8.1.2 Highly modified water courses and canals

**Definition:**

Natural water courses highly modified due to man-made interventions
(usually concrete bedding and or straightened river shores).

Artificial water courses according to Water Framework Directive[^28]
definition: “water bodies which have been created in a location where no
water body existed before and which have not been created by the direct
physical alteration, movement or realignment of an existing water body”.

**This category includes:**

- Highly modified natural water courses and artificial water courses
  $> 10$ m width.
- Inner harbour areas.

**This category excludes:**

- Natural courses partially modified (e.g. flood control structures as
  bank modification). $\rightarrow$ Other river classes

<div>

</div>

**Appearance:**

(Aquaculture areas), `7101` (Fishing ports), `7102` (Marinas), `7103`
(Shipyards), `9210` (Beaches, dunes and sand plains), `9220` (Sparsely
vegetated areas), and `9230` (Salt marshes).

The background imagery shows dark blue for large water bodies, lighter
blue/turquoise for smaller or modified water features, and reddish/pink
tones for artificial and built-up land surfaces, contrasted with
yellowish/greenish tones for other land cover types. No scale bar,
compass orientation, legend, or reference year is visible on the map.

Exceptions from MMU:

Exceptions from MMU $> 0.5$ ha are made for “8.1.2” in order to keep the
network formed by these linear features.

MMW exceptions:

To maintain continuity of linear features, the MMW may fall below the
limit of 10 m over a distance of up to 100m.

#### 8.1.3 Seasonally connected water courses (oxbows)

**Definition:**

Lentic backwaters as oxbow lakes, dead side arms, abandoned braid, etc.
separated from the main river channels.

**This category includes:**

- Permanent and temporarily separated water bodies belonging to the
  river system.

**This category excludes:**

- Oxbow lakes, dead side arms, meander scar, abandoned braid, etc. not
  water-bearing at the date of the image. $\rightarrow$ map according to
  current land cover.
- Secondary river channels. $\rightarrow$ 8.1.1 Natural & semi-natural
  water courses.

### 8.2 Lakes and reservoirs

A specific decision tree has been elaborated to distinguish the 8.2
Lakes and reservoirs classes from other water classes.

<img
src="Protected_Areas_2021-2024_Nomenclature_Guideline_v1-media/img-fc054c04f46ef77ecfebae49d6198510.png"
data-fig-alt="This is a decision tree diagram for the classification of water bodies designated as &#39;8.2 Lakes and reservoirs&#39; within the Copernicus Land Monitoring Service (CLMS) nomenclature, likely part of the CORINE Land Cover (CLC) or CLC+ system. The classification process starts by evaluating a water body from the &#39;8.2 Lakes and reservoirs&#39; category: 1. **First Decision Point: Is the water body &#39;Natural or naturalized?&#39;** * **IF &#39;Natural&#39;:** * **Second Decision Point: Is it an &#39;Oxbows or dead side-arms?&#39;** * **IF Yes:** Classify the water body as &#39;8.1.3 Seasonally connected water courses (oxbows)&#39;. * **IF No:** Classify the water body as &#39;8.2.1 Natural lakes&#39;. * **IF &#39;Naturalized&#39;:** Classify the water body as &#39;8.2.1 Natural lakes&#39;. 2. **Definition of &#39;8.2.1 Natural lakes&#39;:** This category includes: * Dams, barrages, and lakes of hydropower stations (which are natural water courses with artificial dams). * Lakes with artificial origin but not completely man-made structures in urban areas (e.g., including parks and golf courses). * Ponds resulting from former extractive industries (e.g., gravel mining). 3. **Third Decision Point (following from the initial &#39;8.2 Lakes and reservoirs&#39; if not &#39;Natural or naturalized&#39;): Is the water body &#39;Artificial?&#39;** * **IF &#39;Water bodies included in industrial sites?&#39;:** Classify as &#39;1.1.2 Industrial, commercial and military units&#39;. * **IF &#39;Completely man-made ponds?&#39;:** Classify as &#39;8.2.2 Reservoirs&#39;. * **IF &#39;Highly modified inland fish ponds?&#39;:** Classify as &#39;8.2.3 Aquaculture ponds&#39;. * **IF &#39;Water bodies in mineral extraction sites or gravel pits?&#39;:** Classify as &#39;8.2.4 Standing water bodies of extractive industrial sites&#39;. * **IF &#39;Inland salines?&#39;:** Classify as &#39;1.3.1 Mineral extraction, dump and construction sites&#39;." />

This is a decision tree diagram for the classification of water bodies
designated as “8.2 Lakes and reservoirs” within the Copernicus Land
Monitoring Service (CLMS) nomenclature, likely part of the CORINE Land
Cover (CLC) or CLC+ system.

The classification process starts by evaluating a water body from the
“8.2 Lakes and reservoirs” category:

1.  **First Decision Point: Is the water body “Natural or
    naturalized?”**
    - **IF “Natural”:**
      - **Second Decision Point: Is it an “Oxbows or dead side-arms?”**
        - **IF Yes:** Classify the water body as “8.1.3 Seasonally
          connected water courses (oxbows)”.
        - **IF No:** Classify the water body as “8.2.1 Natural lakes”.
    - **IF “Naturalized”:** Classify the water body as “8.2.1 Natural
      lakes”.
2.  **Definition of “8.2.1 Natural lakes”:** This category includes:
    - Dams, barrages, and lakes of hydropower stations (which are
      natural water courses with artificial dams).
    - Lakes with artificial origin but not completely man-made
      structures in urban areas (e.g., including parks and golf
      courses).
    - Ponds resulting from former extractive industries (e.g., gravel
      mining).
3.  **Third Decision Point (following from the initial “8.2 Lakes and
    reservoirs” if not ‘Natural or naturalized’): Is the water body
    “Artificial?”**
    - **IF “Water bodies included in industrial sites?”:** Classify as
      “1.1.2 Industrial, commercial and military units”.
    - **IF “Completely man-made ponds?”:** Classify as “8.2.2
      Reservoirs”.
    - **IF “Highly modified inland fish ponds?”:** Classify as “8.2.3
      Aquaculture ponds”.
    - **IF “Water bodies in mineral extraction sites or gravel pits?”:**
      Classify as “8.2.4 Standing water bodies of extractive industrial
      sites”.
    - **IF “Inland salines?”:** Classify as “1.3.1 Mineral extraction,
      dump and construction sites”.

#### 8.2.1 Natural lakes

**Definition:**

Natural permanent lakes, including dams, barrages and lakes of
hydropower stations. Includes lakes with artificial origin in urban
environments and lakes resulting from former extraction industries
(gravel mining, open cast pit) after restoration.

**This category includes:**

- Lakes, ponds and pools of natural origin containing fresh water.
- Lakes, ponds and pools of artificial origin but with natural
  structure.
- Dams, barrages and lakes of hydropower stations (including their
  tails).
- Lake with artificial origin in urban environments.
- Lakes resulting from former extractive industries (gravel mining, open
  cast pit) after restoration.
- Water surface of temporary ponds visible at time of satellite
  acquisition date.
- Floating aquatic vegetation.

**This category excludes:**

- Fish ponds $\rightarrow$ 8.2.3 Aquaculture ponds.
- Ponds and lakes with completely man-made structure $\rightarrow$ 8.2.2
  Reservoirs.
- Concrete structure and related areas in dams, barrages and lakes of
  hydropower stations will be classified as 1.1.2 Industrial, commercial
  and military units. Soil removed in these sites will be classified as
  1.3.1 Mineral extraction, dump and construction sites. Watercourses
  passing power stations and dams will be classified as 8.1.2 Highly
  modified water courses and canals.
- Flooded areas e.g. due to heavy rain conditions etc. $\rightarrow$ map
  according to current land cover.

<div>

</div>

**Appearance:**

src="Protected_Areas_2021-2024_Nomenclature_Guideline_v1-media/img-ad27212785b5658f21750ba9feb6d187.jpeg"
style="width:320pt"

Two clusters of reddish-pink pixels indicate human settlements; one larger cluster is situated in the upper left of the image, and a smaller one is located closer to the center, adjacent to a bend in the water body. The main water body&#39;s irregular shoreline is delineated by an overlaid light blue-green boundary line. Additionally, a distinct red line traces a path or feature across the brownish land area to the east of the water body. The surrounding terrain appears undulating. This image serves as a visual example for the classification of &#39;Natural lakes&#39; in land cover mapping programmes, such as the Copernicus Land Monitoring Service (CLMS) CORINE Land Cover (CLC+) programme, encompassing features like lakes, dams, barrages, and hydropower station reservoirs."

<div>

</div>

#### 8.2.2 Reservoirs

**Definition:**

Pond with completely man-made structure. Water reservoirs, especially in
Mediterranean countries, used for irrigation and located in agricultural
surroundings. This category includes ponds and water basins for
industrial use/sewage not connected to buildings and other facilities
like storage tanks.

Main characteristic are rectangular or circular shape, concrete border,
sometimes fenced.

**This category includes:**

- Completely artificial ponds used for irrigation or industrial deposits
  (sedimentation ponds, sewage ponds, storm water ponds or water ponds
  near ski areas as a source for artificial snow).
- Other man-made liquid pools not connected with buildings or tanks.

**This category excludes:**

- Natural or naturalized ponds. $\rightarrow$ 8.2.1 Natural lakes.
- Lakes in urban areas (parks, recreational and sport lakes) with
  artificial origin $\rightarrow$ 8.2.1 Natural lakes.
- Water ponds/lakes in industrial sites $\rightarrow$ 1.1.2 Industrial,
  commercial and military units.
- Lakes that originate from former extractive industries $\rightarrow$
  8.2.4 Standing water bodies of extractive industrial sites.
- Fish ponds $\rightarrow$ 8.2.3 Aquaculture ponds.

<div>

</div>

**Appearance:**

<div>

</div>

#### 8.2.3 Aquaculture ponds

**Definition:**

Highly transformed and controlled freshwater ponds, artificial lakes or
reservoirs that are stocked with fish and are used in aquaculture and
fish farming.

**This category includes:**

- Highly transformed ponds used as fish farms / for fish farming.

**This category excludes:**

- Natural water bodies used as fish farms / for fish farming.
  $\rightarrow$ 8.2.1 Natural lakes.
- Highly transformed coastal waters used mainly for fish-breeding
  activities $\rightarrow$ 7.2.2 Salines or 8.3.1 Lagoons if fish farms
  are located in coastal lagoons.
- Completely man-made ponds with concrete structure $\rightarrow$ Class
  8.2.2 Reservoirs.
- Silted basins that are not used any more $\rightarrow$ map current
  land cover.
- Fish cage systems in lakes, reservoirs or in the sea.

<div>

#### 8.2.4 Standing water bodies of extractive industrial sites

**Definition:**

Water bodies related to active quarry created from ground water or
rainwater accumulation.

**This category includes:**

- Water bodies in active gravel pits.
- Water bodies associated with open pit extraction of gravel.
- Decanting pools associated with mining activities.
- Toxic lakes used for disposal (only if additional information
  indicates industrial purposes – if no information is available: 8.2.1
  Natural lakes or 8.2.2 Reservoirs).

**This category excludes:**

- Mineral treatment pools in mine facilities with industrial liquids and
  mud. $\rightarrow$ 8.2.2 Reservoirs.
- Water bodies associated to waste treatment plants. $\rightarrow$ 8.2.2
  Reservoirs.
- Liquid pools in industrial facilities $\rightarrow$ 1.1.2 Industrial,
  commercial and military units.

### 8.3 Transitional waters

#### 8.3.1 Lagoons

**Definition:**

Lagoons (8.3.1) are, according to the EUNIS habitat classification,
characterized by their chemical composition (presence of salt water) and
their morphology (partially separated from the sea by sand banks or
shingle, or, less frequently, by rocks). Coastal lagoons are vegetated
(reed bed and salt marsh plants) or unvegetated.

CORINE Land Cover 521 Coastal lagoons is adopted: “Stretches of salt or
brackish water in coastal areas which are separated from the sea by a
tongue of land or other similar topography. These water bodies can be
connected to the sea at limited points, either permanently or for parts
of the year only”[^29].

Coastal lagoons are considered as a water ecosystem composed by water
and specific adapted vegetation (reed beds and marsh plants). Coastal
lagoons also appear without or less vegetation coverage.

This category is relevant in Mediterranean coastal areas. This class is
also present in Baltic sea and is a scarce habitat at the Atlantic
coast.

**This category includes:**

- Open water of coastal lagoons.
- Reed beds in coastal lagoons
- Floating aquatic vegetation in coastal lagoons.
- Tidal mud flats and muddy areas in coastal lagoons.
- Tidal channels.
- Areas separated by barriers in coastal lagoons and not included in
  salines or fish farms.
- Coastal lagoons separated from the sea by rocks if they are
  distinguishable from fjords and other related features. This type is
  common for the coasts of Scotland, Wales and Ireland (silted or
  chocked coastal lagoons).
- Percolation lagoons if they are clearly separated from sea by sand
  banks or shingle.
- Coastal lagoons separated from the sea by artificial structures, as
  roads.

**This category excludes:**

- Estuarine lagoon (a lagoon interconnected to a major river)
  $\rightarrow$ 8.3.2 Estuaries.
- Inland salt marshes $\rightarrow$ 7.1.1 Inland marshes.
- Coastal salt marshes $\rightarrow$ 7.2.1. Salt marshes
- Water courses $\rightarrow$ 8.1.x Water courses.
- Beaches and dunes $\rightarrow$ 6.2.x Beaches, dunes, sands.
- Fjords and other coastal lagoons not separated from the sea by a
  visible tongue of land, are not included $\rightarrow$ 8.4.0 Sea and
  ocean.

<div>

</div>

**Appearance:**

- Vegetationless (water coverage) bodies of water generally separated
  from the sea by a land barrier (usually sand).

<div>

</div>

The following types of coastal lagoons are considered according to their
main morphological characteristics:

- Isolated lagoons: These are shallow bodies of water separated from the
  sea by a sand barrier (dunes and beaches). These lagoons are connected
  to the sea at limited points. Typical for Mediterranean coastal areas.
  In Spain called *Albuferas*.

<img
src="Protected_Areas_2021-2024_Nomenclature_Guideline_v1-media/img-8d44c66f911c222aeb7e27034a92394a.png"
style="width:352pt"
data-fig-alt="This schematic map illustrates the land cover and land use (LULC) types within a typical coastal lagoon landscape, using a colour-coded legend and CORINE Land Cover (CLC) type numerical codes. The map features a large central light blue area labelled &#39;8.3.1 Lagoons&#39;, representing a &#39;Coastal lagoon&#39;. This lagoon is separated from the &#39;Sea and Ocean&#39; (dark blue, labelled &#39;8.4.2 Coastal waters&#39;) by a narrow land barrier. This barrier comprises a yellow &#39;Sand bank&#39; and dark olive green &#39;Vegetated dunes&#39;, which are collectively labelled &#39;6.2.1 Beaches and Dunes&#39;. Orange arrows indicate connections or flows across this barrier. Inland from the lagoon, there are large light green areas labelled &#39;2.1.1 Arable irrigated and non-irrigated land&#39;. The legend identifies the light green as &#39;Rice&#39; and a paler green/white as &#39;Irrigated land&#39;; both types fall under the &#39;2.1.1 Arable irrigated and non-irrigated land&#39; classification. Adjacent to the vegetated dunes and other arable land, a dark green area is labelled &#39;3.2.1 Other natural &amp; semi-natural coniferous forest&#39;, which corresponds to &#39;Pine&#39; in the legend. A small light green patch, classified as &#39;5.3 Sclerophyllous scrub&#39;, is also present inland. The legend provides the following colour-to-land cover type mapping: * Light blue: Coastal lagoon * Yellow: Sand bank * Dark olive green: Vegetated dunes * Dark green: Pine * Light green: Rice * White/Pale green: Irrigated land * Dark blue: Sea and Ocean"
alt="Schematic view of La Albufera coastal lagoon (Valencia, Spain)." />

This schematic map illustrates the land cover and land use (LULC) types
within a typical coastal lagoon landscape, using a colour-coded legend
and CORINE Land Cover (CLC) type numerical codes. The map features a
large central light blue area labelled “8.3.1 Lagoons”, representing a
“Coastal lagoon”. This lagoon is separated from the “Sea and Ocean”
(dark blue, labelled “8.4.2 Coastal waters”) by a narrow land barrier.
This barrier comprises a yellow “Sand bank” and dark olive green
“Vegetated dunes”, which are collectively labelled “6.2.1 Beaches and
Dunes”. Orange arrows indicate connections or flows across this barrier.

Inland from the lagoon, there are large light green areas labelled
“2.1.1 Arable irrigated and non-irrigated land”. The legend identifies
the light green as “Rice” and a paler green/white as “Irrigated land”;
both types fall under the “2.1.1 Arable irrigated and non-irrigated
land” classification. Adjacent to the vegetated dunes and other arable
land, a dark green area is labelled “3.2.1 Other natural & semi-natural
coniferous forest”, which corresponds to “Pine” in the legend. A small
light green patch, classified as “5.3 Sclerophyllous scrub”, is also
present inland.

The legend provides the following colour-to-land cover type mapping: \*
Light blue: Coastal lagoon \* Yellow: Sand bank \* Dark olive green:
Vegetated dunes \* Dark green: Pine \* Light green: Rice \* White/Pale
green: Irrigated land \* Dark blue: Sea and Ocean

In many cases, these coastal lagoons which are separated by sand
barriers are open to the sea due to sea erosion or other processes
(leaky, restricted or open type lagoons). In other cases they are
entirely surrounded by sand banks.

The map shows a light green area labelled &#39;2.1.1 Arable irrigated and non-irrigated land&#39; bordering a large body of light blue water labelled &#39;8.3.1 Lagoons&#39;. bank”. \* Light green represents “Irrigated land”. \* Dark blue

defines four classes: - Light blue represents “Lagoon”, corresponding to
the area labelled “8.3.1 Lagoons” which is the primary water body in the

to the land barrier labelled “6.2.1 Beaches and Dunes” that partially

The diagram shows the “8.3.1 Lagoons” (light blue) separated from the
“Sea and Ocean” (dark blue) by a narrow land barrier. This barrier is
primarily composed of “6.2.1 Beaches and Dunes” (beige), with a small

Many of these lagoons are either man-made OR artificially transformed
(especially by the construction of roads on the sand barriers).

data-fig-alt="This schematic map illustrates a typical coastal lagoon landscape, depicting various land cover and land use (LULC) classifications. The main geographic features and their classifications are: * **Light blue (large areas):** &#39;8.3.1 Lagoons,&#39; representing the primary water body. * **Dark blue (large areas):** &#39;8.4.2 Coastal waters&#39; and &#39;6.2.1 Beaches and Dunes,&#39; generally labelled as &#39;Sea and ocean.&#39; * **Yellow:** &#39;Sand bank,&#39; forming a barrier between the lagoon and the sea. * **Red:** &#39;1.1.1 Urban fabric,&#39; also labelled as &#39;Urban area,&#39; situated on the land barrier. * **Olive green:** &#39;5.3 Sclerophyllous scrub,&#39; appearing on both the mainland and near the coastal barrier. * **Light green:** &#39;2.1.1 Arable land,&#39; also labelled as &#39;Irrigated land,&#39; located inland from the lagoon. * **Thin light blue lines:** &#39;Roads,&#39; traversing the landscape. Inland, agricultural areas (2.1.1) are present."

This schematic map illustrates a typical coastal lagoon landscape,
depicting various land cover and land use (LULC) classifications. The
map uses distinct colours to represent different features, with
corresponding CORINE Land Cover (CLC) codes and labels shown directly on
the map and in the legend.

The main geographic features and their classifications are: \* **Light
blue (large areas):** “8.3.1 Lagoons,” representing the primary water
body. \* **Dark blue (large areas):** “8.4.2 Coastal waters” and “6.2.1
Beaches and Dunes,” generally labelled as “Sea and ocean.” \*
**Yellow:** “Sand bank,” forming a barrier between the lagoon and the
sea. \* **Red:** “1.1.1 Urban fabric,” also labelled as “Urban area,”
situated on the land barrier. \* **Olive green:** “5.3 Sclerophyllous
scrub,” appearing on both the mainland and near the coastal barrier. \*
**Light green:** “2.1.1 Arable land,” also labelled as “Irrigated land,”
located inland from the lagoon. \* **Thin light blue lines:** “Roads,”
traversing the landscape.

The spatial arrangement shows a lagoon (8.3.1) separated from the sea
and ocean (8.4.2, 6.2.1) by a land mass that includes sand banks, urban
fabric (1.1.1), and sclerophyllous scrub (5.3). Inland, agricultural
areas (2.1.1) are present.

Percolation lagoons[^30]:

These are normally separated from the sea by sand or shingle banks.
Seawater enters by percolating through the bank.

This map provides a schematic representation of a coastal lagoon
landscape, illustrating various land cover/land use types using a
colour-coded legend and numerical codes, likely from a CORINE Land Cover
(CLC) related nomenclature. The legend defines six categories: \* Light
blue represents “Lagoon” (coded 8.3.1 Lagoons). \* Yellow represents
“Sand bank” (coded 6.2.1.0 Beaches). \* Dark olive green represents
“Vegetated dunes” (coded 5.1.0 Heathland and moorland). \* Light beige
represents “Arable land” (coded 2.1.1 Arable land). \* Light grey
represents “Cliffs” (coded 6.3.1.1 Bare). \* Dark blue represents “Sea
and ocean” (coded 8.4.2 Coastal waters).

The map depicts a large “8.3.1 Lagoons” (light blue) area separated from
“8.4.2 Coastal waters” (dark blue) by a distinct yellow “6.2.1.0
Beaches” (Sand bank) barrier. Inland from this barrier, a dark olive
green strip is labelled “5.1.0 Heathland and moorland” (Vegetated
dunes). The vast majority of the terrestrial area surrounding the lagoon
and coastal features is light beige, designated “2.1.1 Arable land”. A
light grey area, labelled “6.3.1.1 Bare” (Cliffs), is located along the
northern edge of the “8.4.2 Coastal waters”. A thin, light blue linear
feature, likely a river or stream, enters the “8.4.2 Coastal waters”
from the north-east, traversing a yellow “Sand bank” area.

A specific case of coastal lagoons are silted or choked lagoons. These
coastal lagoons are open to the sea, but the water is retained by a rock
barrier (in many cases not visible in the satellite images) or by a long
narrow entrance channel. Fjords are the same landforms that only differ
from lagoons in their water depth.

<img
src="Protected_Areas_2021-2024_Nomenclature_Guideline_v1-media/img-19f3d743797f2d8526cb9c68454fb934.png"
style="width:311pt"
data-fig-alt="This schematic map illustrates a generic coastal lagoon environment, depicting distinct land cover and water body types, consistent with the Copernicus Land Monitoring Service (CLMS) and CORINE Land Cover (CLC) nomenclature guidelines. While not tied to a specific named location, it represents features found in areas such as La Albufera (Spain) or Logarou lake (Greece). The map uses four distinct colour codes with corresponding labels: * **Light blue** areas represent &#39;Lago&#39; which corresponds to class &#39;8.3.1 Lagoons&#39;. This includes a large central lagoon and a narrower, elongated water body extending southwest. * **Light grey** areas represent &#39;Muddy&#39; zones, depicted as narrow strips along the land-water interface within the lagoon system, and are also considered part of class 8.3.1 Lagoons. * **Dark blue** areas represent &#39;Sea and [unreadable, likely &#39;Ocean&#39; or &#39;Coastal water&#39;]&#39;, labelled as &#39;8.4.2 Coastal&#39;, situated to the east of a separating land barrier. * **Beige** areas represent &#39;Lan[d, unreadable]&#39;, with the extensive land area to the west of the lagoon explicitly labelled as &#39;2.1.1 Arable irrigated and [unreadable, likely &#39;permanent crops&#39;]&#39;. This beige colour also forms the narrow barrier separating the main lagoon from the open sea. The spatial pattern shows a large, irregularly shaped lagoon (8.3.1) partially enclosed by land (2.1.1 Arable irrigated) and separated from the open sea (8.4.2 Coastal) by a land barrier. Muddy areas are present along the lagoon&#39;s edges, highlighting transitional aquatic zones. No scale bar, compass, or specific reference year is provided."
alt="Schematic view of silled lagoon of Strangford Lough (Northern Ireland, United Kindgdom)." />

This schematic map illustrates a generic coastal lagoon environment,
depicting distinct land cover and water body types, consistent with the
Copernicus Land Monitoring Service (CLMS) and CORINE Land Cover (CLC)
nomenclature guidelines. While not tied to a specific named location, it
represents features found in areas such as La Albufera (Spain) or
Logarou lake (Greece).

The map uses four distinct colour codes with corresponding labels: \*
**Light blue** areas represent “Lago” which corresponds to class “8.3.1
Lagoons”. This includes a large central lagoon and a narrower, elongated
water body extending southwest. \* **Light grey** areas represent
“Muddy” zones, depicted as narrow strips along the land-water interface
within the lagoon system, and are also considered part of class 8.3.1
Lagoons. \* **Dark blue** areas represent “Sea and \[unreadable, likely
‘Ocean’ or ‘Coastal water’\]”, labelled as “8.4.2 Coastal”, situated to
the east of a separating land barrier. \* **Beige** areas represent
“Lan\[d, unreadable\]”, with the extensive land area to the west of the
lagoon explicitly labelled as “2.1.1 Arable irrigated and \[unreadable,
likely ‘permanent crops’\]”. This beige colour also forms the narrow
barrier separating the main lagoon from the open sea.

The spatial pattern shows a large, irregularly shaped lagoon (8.3.1)
partially enclosed by land (2.1.1 Arable irrigated) and separated from
the open sea (8.4.2 Coastal) by a land barrier. Muddy areas are present
along the lagoon’s edges, highlighting transitional aquatic zones. No
scale bar, compass, or specific reference year is provided.

Vegetated (water coverage) bodies of water generally separated from the
sea by a land barrier (usually sand).

<img
src="Protected_Areas_2021-2024_Nomenclature_Guideline_v1-media/img-bac4e0670afcb5dc1df29a2e0d77aa36.jpeg"
style="width:425pt"
data-fig-alt="False-colour satellite image of Limni Kiknolakka, an open coastal lagoon in Greece, illustrating the classification of lagoon features within a Natura 2000 site boundary. The image displays a large, dark blue body of &#39;Open water visible in imagery&#39;, which corresponds to the &#39;8.3.1 Lagoons (without reeds)&#39; classification. Surrounding the open water, areas of dark green and reddish-brown vegetation are identified as &#39;Reed coverage&#39;, correlating to the &#39;8.3.1 Lagoons (with reeds)&#39; classification. A thin green line delineates the &#39;Border of Natura 2000 (N2K) site&#39;, encompassing the lagoon and adjacent land areas. The surrounding land shows a mosaic of red (likely agricultural land) and lighter tones (possibly bare ground or built-up areas). No scale bar, compass, or specific date is visible."
alt="Limnes Voulkaria Kai Saltini Natura 2000 site (Greece), Spot 5 (2.5 m; 1/2/3 band combination). Date: 2011-02-05. CNES 2011©, Distribution Airbus DS/Spot Image." />

False-colour satellite image of Limni Kiknolakka, an open coastal lagoon
in Greece, illustrating the classification of lagoon features within a
Natura 2000 site boundary. The image displays a large, dark blue body of
“Open water visible in imagery”, which corresponds to the “8.3.1 Lagoons
(without reeds)” classification. Surrounding the open water, areas of
dark green and reddish-brown vegetation are identified as “Reed
coverage”, correlating to the “8.3.1 Lagoons (with reeds)”
classification. A thin green line delineates the “Border of Natura 2000
(N2K) site”, encompassing the lagoon and adjacent land areas. The
surrounding land shows a mosaic of red (likely agricultural land) and
lighter tones (possibly bare ground or built-up areas). No scale bar,
compass, or specific date is visible.

This class only applies to following situations in coastal lagoons not
related with estuaries or coastal wetlands:

- A. Reed beds and marsh plants inside coastal lagoons.
- B. Reed beds and marsh plants located in the shoreline of coastal
  lagoons.
- C. Reed beds and marsh plants dividing two coastal lagoons.
- D. Reed beds and marsh plants separated from coastal lagoons not
  related with estuaries or coastal lagoons.

<img
src="Protected_Areas_2021-2024_Nomenclature_Guideline_v1-media/img-e153dc12fa914c68e4bfa5058e383d36.jpg"
style="width:309pt"
data-fig-alt="This schematic map illustrates a coastal lagoon environment, detailing various land cover types and their spatial arrangement. The legend defines five categories: &#39;Coastal lagoon&#39; (light blue), &#39;Sand bank&#39; (yellow), &#39;Reed beds and marsh plants&#39; (grey areas with green vertical plant symbols), &#39;Sea and ocean&#39; (dark blue), and &#39;Irrigated land&#39; (light green). The main body of water, a coastal lagoon, is depicted in light blue and is connected to the dark blue &#39;Sea and ocean&#39; on the right side by a narrow, elongated yellow &#39;Sand bank&#39;. The coastal lagoon is extensively surrounded by light green &#39;Irrigated land&#39; to its left and top. Within the coastal lagoon and along its edges, several distinct areas of &#39;Reed beds and marsh plants&#39; are shown in grey with green plant symbols, marked with labels A, B, C, and D. Area A is a smaller patch of reed beds and marsh plants within the lagoon. Areas B and C are larger, more complex formations of reed beds and marsh plants situated along the interface between the irrigated land and the lagoon on the left side. Area D is a smaller, isolated patch of reed beds and marsh plants on the top right, bordering both the irrigated land and the coastal lagoon."
alt="Concept of coastal marshes with reeds: A, B, C and D situations are considered as 8.3.1 Lagoons." />

This schematic map illustrates a coastal lagoon environment, detailing
various land cover types and their spatial arrangement. The legend
defines five categories: “Coastal lagoon” (light blue), “Sand bank”
(yellow), “Reed beds and marsh plants” (grey areas with green vertical
plant symbols), “Sea and ocean” (dark blue), and “Irrigated land” (light
green). The main body of water, a coastal lagoon, is depicted in light
blue and is connected to the dark blue “Sea and ocean” on the right side
by a narrow, elongated yellow “Sand bank”. The coastal lagoon is
extensively surrounded by light green “Irrigated land” to its left and
top. Within the coastal lagoon and along its edges, several distinct
areas of “Reed beds and marsh plants” are shown in grey with green plant
symbols, marked with labels A, B, C, and D. Area A is a smaller patch of
reed beds and marsh plants within the lagoon. Areas B and C are larger,
more complex formations of reed beds and marsh plants situated along the
interface between the irrigated land and the lagoon on the left side.
Area D is a smaller, isolated patch of reed beds and marsh plants on the
top right, bordering both the irrigated land and the coastal lagoon.

In the case of complex coastal lagoons (costal lagoons related to
coastal wetlands and estuaries) they will be classified as 7.2.1 Salt
marshes, prevailing the concept of coastal wetlands over coastal
lagoons. In these cases, only reed beds and marsh plants inside the
waterbody or/ and separating two coastal lagoons will be classified as
8.3.1 Lagoons.

<img
src="Protected_Areas_2021-2024_Nomenclature_Guideline_v1-media/img-0a82934073b9bc6cfb34b10202bfae7c.png"
style="width:311pt"
data-fig-alt="This schematic map illustrates various land cover and habitat types typically found within and adjacent to a coastal lagoon ecosystem. The map uses distinct colours to represent different features, corresponding to a classification system. The legend defines the following colour-coded features: * Light blue: Lagoon (labelled with code 8.3.1) * Yellow: Sand bank, and Beaches and Dunes (labelled with code 6.2.1) * Grey: Reed beds and marsh plants, encompassing Salt marshes (labelled with code 7.2.1) and Natural &amp; semi-natural water courses (labelled with code 8.1.1) * Light blue lines within grey areas: Rivers * Dark blue: Sea and ocean, including Coastal waters (labelled with code 8.4.2) * Light green: Irrigated land, and Arable irrigated and non-irrigated land (labelled with code 2.1.1) * Dark olive green: Sclerophyllous scrub (labelled with code 5.3) The spatial pattern shows a large central Lagoon (8.3.1) connected to the Sea and ocean (8.4.2 Coastal waters) via an opening in a long, yellow Sand bank (6.2.1 Beaches and Dunes). A river system (light blue lines within 8.1.1 Natural &amp; semi-natural water courses) flows into the lagoon area, surrounded by grey areas of Salt marshes (7.2.1) and Reed beds and marsh plants. Inland, the landscape transitions to areas of Sclerophyllous scrub (5.3) and Arable irrigated and non-irrigated land (2.1.1). A smaller section of Beaches and Dunes (6.2.1) is also visible along the coast. No scale bar, compass, or specific geographic location is provided, indicating this is a conceptual diagram."
alt="Schematic concept for differentiation between 8.3.1 class." />

This schematic map illustrates various land cover and habitat types
typically found within and adjacent to a coastal lagoon ecosystem. The
map uses distinct colours to represent different features, corresponding
to a classification system.

The legend defines the following colour-coded features: \* Light blue:
Lagoon (labelled with code 8.3.1) \* Yellow: Sand bank, and Beaches and
Dunes (labelled with code 6.2.1) \* Grey: Reed beds and marsh plants,
encompassing Salt marshes (labelled with code 7.2.1) and Natural &
semi-natural water courses (labelled with code 8.1.1) \* Light blue
lines within grey areas: Rivers \* Dark blue: Sea and ocean, including
Coastal waters (labelled with code 8.4.2) \* Light green: Irrigated
land, and Arable irrigated and non-irrigated land (labelled with code
2.1.1) \* Dark olive green: Sclerophyllous scrub (labelled with code
5.3)

The spatial pattern shows a large central Lagoon (8.3.1) connected to
the Sea and ocean (8.4.2 Coastal waters) via an opening in a long,
yellow Sand bank (6.2.1 Beaches and Dunes). A river system (light blue
lines within 8.1.1 Natural & semi-natural water courses) flows into the
lagoon area, surrounded by grey areas of Salt marshes (7.2.1) and Reed
beds and marsh plants. Inland, the landscape transitions to areas of
Sclerophyllous scrub (5.3) and Arable irrigated and non-irrigated land
(2.1.1). A smaller section of Beaches and Dunes (6.2.1) is also visible
along the coast. No scale bar, compass, or specific geographic location
is provided, indicating this is a conceptual diagram.

Special cases are coastal lagoons covered by reed beds and marsh plants
and the presence of tidal channel. In these cases the tidal channels
will be as well classified as 8.3.1 Lagoons.

<img
src="Protected_Areas_2021-2024_Nomenclature_Guideline_v1-media/img-ac1c7f8a1e3098bfcfe09a241af460c8.png"
style="width:315pt"
data-fig-alt="A schematic map illustrating the spatial arrangement of coastal lagoons and their surrounding land cover types, likely representing CLC+ (Copernicus Land Cover / Land Use) class 8.3.1 Lagoons. The legend defines the following features by colour: * Cyan: Lagoon (labelled &#39;8.3.1 Lagoons&#39; in two locations) * Yellow: Sand bank * Grey: Reed beds and marsh plants * Pale blue: Rivers * Dark blue: Sea and ocean * Light green: Irrigated land The map depicts a dark blue &#39;Sea and ocean&#39; area along its outer edge, which is partially separated from a complex network of internal &#39;Lagoons&#39; (cyan) by a yellow &#39;Sand bank&#39;. The lagoons are connected to the sea through an opening in the sand bank. Several pale blue &#39;Rivers&#39; flow into these lagoons. The land areas immediately surrounding the lagoons and rivers are composed of grey &#39;Reed beds and marsh plants&#39;, while a light green area representing &#39;Irrigated land&#39; is visible further inland. This spatial pattern highlights how sand banks act as barriers between open sea and lagoon systems, which are fed by rivers and surrounded by specific wetland vegetation and agricultural land."
alt="Schematic concept of coastal lagoons (with reed beds) and tide channels (Leaky coastal lagoon of Ria Formosa in Portugal)." />

and their surrounding land cover types, likely representing CLC+
(Copernicus Land Cover / Land Use) class 8.3.1 Lagoons. The legend
defines the following features by colour: \* Cyan: Lagoon (labelled
“8.3.1 Lagoons” in two locations) \* Yellow: Sand bank \* Grey: Reed
beds and marsh plants \* Pale blue: Rivers \* Dark blue: Sea and ocean
\* Light green: Irrigated land The map depicts a dark blue ‘Sea and
ocean’ area along its outer edge, which is partially separated from a
complex network of internal ‘Lagoons’ (cyan) by a yellow ‘Sand bank’.
The lagoons are connected to the sea through an opening in the sand
bank. Several pale blue ‘Rivers’ flow into these lagoons. The land areas
immediately surrounding the lagoons and rivers are composed of grey
‘Reed beds and marsh plants’, while a light green area representing
‘Irrigated land’ is visible further inland. This spatial pattern
highlights how sand banks act as barriers between open sea and lagoon
systems, which are fed by rivers and surrounded by specific wetland
vegetation and agricultural land.

**Methodological advice:**

- Use of CLC class 5.2.13 as orientation.

#### 8.3.2 Estuaries

**Definition:**

CORINE Land Cover 522 Estuaries class is adopted: “The mouth of a river
within which the tide ebbs and flows, either permanently or for parts of
the year only”[^31].

In many cases, estuaries are associated with marshes.

**This category includes:**

- Estuaries.
- Deltas.

**This category excludes:**

- Marshes in estuaries $\rightarrow$ 7.2.1 Salt marshes
- Coastal waters $\rightarrow$ 8.4.2 Coastal waters.
- Coastal lagoons $\rightarrow$ 8.3.1 Lagoons.
- Rivers ending in highly artificial harbors will not be categorized as
  8.3.2 Estuaries.
- Fjords, *rias* (in Northwestern Spain) and straits $\rightarrow$ 8.3.3
  Marine inlets and fjords.

<img
src="Protected_Areas_2021-2024_Nomenclature_Guideline_v1-media/img-606374361576b858a5d5f1fccd2a3435.jpeg"
style="width:309pt"
data-fig-alt="A ground-level photograph captures a serene landscape featuring a broad expanse of calm, brownish-tinted water under a light blue sky with scattered white cumulus clouds. Numerous small, light-coloured boats are visible, widely distributed across the water surface. Along the distant horizon, a low, dark green line of land, possibly vegetated with trees, is discernible. This image represents a typical coastal lagoon or estuary environment, aligning with CORINE Land Cover (CLC) class 8.3.1 Lagoons or 8.3.2 Estuaries, and is consistent with descriptions of locations like the Ria Formosa in Portugal."

A ground-level photograph captures a serene landscape featuring a broad
expanse of calm, brownish-tinted water under a light blue sky with
scattered white cumulus clouds. Numerous small, light-coloured boats are
visible, widely distributed across the water surface. Along the distant
horizon, a low, dark green line of land, possibly vegetated with trees,
is discernible. This image represents a typical coastal lagoon or
estuary environment, aligning with CORINE Land Cover (CLC) class 8.3.1
Lagoons or 8.3.2 Estuaries, and is consistent with descriptions of
locations like the Ria Formosa in Portugal.

**Appearance:**

- Ending of rivers which flow into the sea.
- Estuaries are influenced by tides and in small rivers sand/mud appears
  when low tide occurs.
- The limit between 8.2.2 Estuaries and 8.4.1 Coastal waters is based on
  the intended continuation of the coastline.

<div>

</div>

In delta rivers, the area where the river meets the sea is classified as
8.3.2 Estuaries. The area affected by tides (presence of mud in the
image) inside the deltaic system which is not covered by vegetation is
also classified as 8.3.2 Estuaries.

morphology in an estuarine environment, using simplified polygons for
four distinct classes. The legend defines the colour coding: \* Grey
(8.3.2): Estuaries, representing the main body of the river where it

estuarine channels and tidal areas. \* Blue (8.4.0): Sea and ocean,
depicting the open water body into which the estuary flows. \* Olive
green (6.1): Sparsely vegetated areas, located further inland or along
the higher banks of the estuary, bordering the salt marshes. The map
shows a branching pattern of estuaries (grey) flowing into the wider sea
and ocean (blue), with extensive areas of salt marshes (pink)
distributed along the estuarine banks and within tidal flats. Sparsely
vegetated areas (olive green) are depicted further inland, adjacent to
the upper reaches of the estuarine arms. This configuration illustrates
the typical transition of land cover types from terrestrial to marine
environments within an estuary.

The limits between 8.1.1 Natural & semi-natural water courses and 8.3.2
Estuaries is based in the following practical visual rules:

<div>

</div>

**Methodological advice:**

- Use of CORINE LC class 5.2.2 as orientation.
- Computer assisted visual interpretation complemented by ancillary
  information. The delimitation of CLC 8.3.2 Estuaries / 8.1 Water
  courses will be determined by the photo interpreter according to
  morphological characteristics as e.g. river junctions or other
  geographic elements as bare or sandy capes.
- Use of Transitional Water layer (European Framework Directive) to
  identify general limits of coastal waters.
- Use of ancillary information:
  - GIO-Land HR Water/Wetland Layers.
  - Natura 2000 database.
  - Wetland Ramsar Sites.
  - Any available ancillary or in-situ data sources.

#### 8.3.3 Marine inlets and fjords

**Definition:**

Marine inlets and fjords are valley-shaped openings in the coastline
stretching inland. Fjords are created through glacier activity where a
valley is carved out and later submerged because of rising sea level.
Marine inlets, e.g. ria coast in north-western Spain, originate from
rivers forming a valley which is flooded due to a rise of sea level.
Both can have a complex structure of branches, which may unite to one
before entering the sea. Water inside marine inlets and fjords are
influenced by the sea and its tide and are therefore saline. Delineation
on the water surface is a straight line between the two points of entry
into the sea on each side of the inlet. In contrast to the often long
and linear shaped fjords, marine inlets can have a very broad funnel
shaped opening to the sea. To differentiate between a marine inlet and a
bay (8.4.2 Coastal waters) a river terminating in the marine inlet or a
dry valley formerly shaped by a river can be used as indicator.

<div>

</div>

**This category includes:**

- Coastal fjords of northern Europe (Scandinavia, Ireland, Scotland,
  Faroe Islands).
- Ria coast in Northern Spain and Andalusia, north-western France,
  Ireland, England and Wales, Croatia, Montenegro and Malta.
- Some kind of lochs in Scotland, if they were carved by rivers or
  glaciers and open into the sea.

**This category excludes:**

- Mouths of rivers $\rightarrow$ 8.1.1 Natural & semi-natural water
  courses.
- Deltas $\rightarrow$ 8.3.2 Estuaries.
- Bays and coastal enclosures by spits $\rightarrow$ 8.4.2 Coastal
  waters.
- Lagoons connected to the sea $\rightarrow$ 8.3.1 Lagoons.
- Tidal inlets and channels $\rightarrow$ depending on size and location
  7.2.1 Salt marshes, 7.2.3 Intertidal flats or 8.4.2 Coastal waters.

**Appearance:**

<div>

</div>

**Methodological advice:**

- Computer assisted visual interpretation complemented by ancillary
  information.

### 8.4 Sea and ocean

#### 8.4.1 Open sea

**Definition:**

Open water of seas and oceans outside coastal waters.

The coastal waters are defined as all water areas extending from the
landside up to one nautical mile into the sea (see detailed description
in 8.4.2 Coastal waters).

**This category includes:**

- Open water outside the coastline, including the Baltic Sea, the
  Bothnia Sea and the Gulf of Bothnia

**This category excludes:**

- Sea area that is within the coastline and one nautical mile seawards
  $\rightarrow$ 8.4.2 Coastal waters

Satellite imagery of a coastal area, likely in false-colour infrared,
illustrating the delineation between “8.4.2.0 Coastal waters” and
“8.4.1.0 Open sea”. The land, visible in the upper portion of the image,
displays urban areas (grey/blue) and vegetated land (red). The immediate
coastal water is light blue/green, transitioning to dark blue/black for
deeper offshore areas. A vertical orange arrow originating from the

boundary between the two water classifications. This arrow is labelled

area extending up to one nautical mile from the landside, with “Open
sea” starting beyond this boundary. No specific geographic location or

**Methodological advice:**

- The mapping of open sea will be an automated GIS operation.

#### 8.4.2 Coastal waters

**Definition:**

Water area between the coastline (or the theoretical coastline in case
of estuaries, inlets, fjords, etc.) and the open sea.

The European water framework directive defines coastal waters as
“surface water on the landward side of a line, every point of which is
at a distance of one nautical mile on the seaward side from the nearest
point of the baseline from which the breadth of territorial waters is
measured, extending where appropriate up to the outer limit of
transitional waters”[^32]. Hence the coastal waters extend from the
landward extent of either the coastal or transitional waters out to one
nautical mile from the baseline. According to the United Nations
Convention on the on the Law of the Sea (UNCLOS) the baseline is
measured as the low-water line except along the mouths of estuaries and
heads of bays where it cuts across open water.[^33]

**This category includes:**

- Open water outside the coastline up to one nautical mile seawards,
  including harbour waters.

**This category excludes:**

- Estuaries. Estuaries are areas greatly affected by freshwater
  influence ($> 20$ m³/s) and variate in water level $\rightarrow$ 8.3.2
  Estuaries.
- Intertidal flats $\rightarrow$ 7.2.3 Intertidal flats.
- Coastal lagoons are areas separated from the sea. These water bodies
  can be connected to the sea at limited points, either permanently or
  for parts of the year only $\rightarrow$ 8.3.1 Lagoons.
- Inlets evolved from valleys created by former glaciers or rivers which
  later submerged, e.g. ria coasts, fjords and some kind of lochs in
  Scotland $\rightarrow$ 8.3.3 Marine inlets and fjords.
- All water outside of one nautical mile $\rightarrow$ 8.4.1 Open sea.

<div>

</div>

**Appearance:**

<div>

</div>

<img
src="Protected_Areas_2021-2024_Nomenclature_Guideline_v1-media/img-730f624167a1d88eeb5ecb14ae5647c4.jpeg"
data-fig-alt="This map displays four conceptual diagrams illustrating the delineation of coastal water boundaries in various geographic settings, relevant for environmental classification such as under the Water Framework Directive (Directive 2000/60/EC). The colour coding represents: green for land, dark blue for open sea or coastal water (defined as open water outside the coastline up to one nautical mile seawards), light blue for inland water bodies (such as estuaries, lagoons, or rivers), grey for intertidal flats or riverine sediment areas, and yellow for specific beach or shoreline zones. A red dotted line consistently indicates the boundary of the open sea/coastal water area relative to the land and other water bodies. The four panels show: 1. **Top-left:** A complex river delta system where light blue river channels and grey intertidal flats extend into the dark blue open sea. The red dotted line follows the highly irregular coastline of the delta and intertidal flats. 2. **Top-right:** A river entering the open sea, featuring a prominent yellow beach area along the coastline. The red dotted line outlines the interface between the green land and yellow beach, and the dark blue open sea, including grey riverine features extending seaward. 3. **Bottom-left:** A large light blue inland water body (likely a lagoon or estuary) connected to the dark blue open sea by a narrow opening. A yellow beach area is visible along the inner part of the coastal zone. The red dotted line delineates the coastal boundary adjacent to the yellow beach and open sea. 4. **Bottom-right:** A bay or inlet scenario where a light blue inland water body with grey areas connects to the dark blue open sea. The red dotted line defines the boundary between the green land and the dark blue open sea along the irregular shoreline of the bay. No scale bar, compass, or specific geographic locations are provided, indicating these are generic illustrative examples for classification guidelines."
alt="Schematic representation of classes 8.4.2 Coastal waters (red dot line)." />

This map displays four conceptual diagrams illustrating the delineation
of coastal water boundaries in various geographic settings, relevant for
environmental classification such as under the Water Framework Directive
(Directive 2000/60/EC). The colour coding represents: green for land,
dark blue for open sea or coastal water (defined as open water outside
the coastline up to one nautical mile seawards), light blue for inland
water bodies (such as estuaries, lagoons, or rivers), grey for
intertidal flats or riverine sediment areas, and yellow for specific
beach or shoreline zones. A red dotted line consistently indicates the
boundary of the open sea/coastal water area relative to the land and
other water bodies.

The four panels show: 1. **Top-left:** A complex river delta system
where light blue river channels and grey intertidal flats extend into
the dark blue open sea. The red dotted line follows the highly irregular
coastline of the delta and intertidal flats. 2. **Top-right:** A river
entering the open sea, featuring a prominent yellow beach area along the
coastline. The red dotted line outlines the interface between the green
land and yellow beach, and the dark blue open sea, including grey
riverine features extending seaward. 3. **Bottom-left:** A large light
blue inland water body (likely a lagoon or estuary) connected to the
dark blue open sea by a narrow opening. A yellow beach area is visible
along the inner part of the coastal zone. The red dotted line delineates
the coastal boundary adjacent to the yellow beach and open sea. 4.
**Bottom-right:** A bay or inlet scenario where a light blue inland
water body with grey areas connects to the dark blue open sea. The red
dotted line defines the boundary between the green land and the dark
blue open sea along the irregular shoreline of the bay.

No scale bar, compass, or specific geographic locations are provided,
indicating these are generic illustrative examples for classification
guidelines.

**Methodological advice:**

- CORINE LC class 5.2.3 as indication.
- The delineation between 8.4.1 Open sea and 8.4.2 Coastal waters will
  be made by semi-automated GIS operations.
- Use of Transitional Waters layer (European Framework Directive) as
  indication of limits between open sea and coastal wetlands/coastal
  lagoons/intertidal flats.
- National data (coastal line from national supporting data such as
  topographical maps is used as a support to define the borderline
  between water courses and coastal sea).
- Supporting remote sensing data and depth data to delineate from 8.2
  Lakes and reservoirs.
- Computer assisted visual interpretation of SPOT-5 data.
